=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums. Your task is to remove all elements from the array by performing one of the following operations at each step until nums is empty:

Choose any two elements from the first three elements of nums and remove them. The cost of this operation is the maximum of the two elements removed.
If fewer than three elements remain in nums, remove all the remaining elements in a single operation. The cost of this operation is the maximum of the remaining elements.

Return the minimum cost required to remove all the elements.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [6,2,8,4]
Output: 12
Explanation:
Initially, nums = [6, 2, 8, 4].

In the first operation, remove nums[0] = 6 and nums[2] = 8 with a cost of max(6, 8) = 8. Now, nums = [2, 4].
In the second operation, remove the remaining elements with a cost of max(2, 4) = 4.

The cost to remove all elements is 8 + 4 = 12. This is the minimum cost to remove all elements in nums. Hence, the output is 12.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [2,1,3,3]
Output: 5
Explanation:
Initially, nums = [2, 1, 3, 3].

In the first operation, remove nums[0] = 2 and nums[1] = 1 with a cost of max(2, 1) = 2. Now, nums = [3, 3].
In the second operation remove the remaining elements with a cost of max(3, 3) = 3.

The cost to remove all elements is 2 + 3 = 5. This is the minimum cost to remove all elements in nums. Hence, the output is 5.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 1000
1 <= nums[i] <= 10^6

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's think about memory/time. n=1000, loops:
for k in range(n, -1, -1):
    if k == n:
       for p in range(n): dp[p][n]=nums[p]
    elif k == n-1:
       for p in range(k): dp[p][k]=max(nums[p], nums[k])
    else:
       for p in range(k):
           dp[p][k] = min(

        for p in range(n):  # p < k = n
            dp[p][k] = nums[p]

                max(nums[k], nums[k+1]) + dp[p][k+2]

return dp[0][1]

Let's ensure final code is syntactically correct:
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        dp = [[0] * (n + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]
        
        for k in range(n, -1, -1):
            if k == n:
                for p in range(n):
                    dp[p][k] = nums[p]
            elif k == n - 1:
                for p in range(k):
                    dp[p][k] = max(nums[p], nums[k])
            else:
                for p in range(k):
                    dp[p][k] = min(
                        max(nums[p], nums[k]) + dp[k + 1][k + 2],
                        max(nums[p], nums[k + 1]) + dp[k][k + 2],
                        max(nums[k], nums[k + 1]) + dp[p][k + 2],
                    )
        return dp[0][1]
```

For n=2: dp 3x3. k=2: p=0,1 dp[0][2]=a, dp[1][2]=b. k=1: k==n-1 (1==1): p in range(1): dp[0][1]=max(a,b). k=0: else? k=0 <= n-2? 0<=0 yes, for p in range(0) none. Return dp[0][1]. Good.

Recurrence:
dp[p][k] depends on dp[p][k+2], dp[k][k+2], dp[k+1][k+2].
So it only depends on column k+2. Thus for each k, we only need column k+2. If we loop k descending by 2? Let's see dependencies preserve parity. We can compute columns of parity n first, then parity n-1? But answer dp[0][1] has parity 1. It depends on column 3, 5, ... up to n if n odd, or n-1 if n even? Let's examine:
- If n is odd, dp[0][1] depends on k=3,5,...,n. So parity odd.
- If n is even, dp[0][1] depends on k=3,5,...,n-1? Because base at k=n-1 (odd) no recurrence. So parity odd. So answer always uses odd k columns. But dp for odd k depends on odd k+2. Thus only odd columns matter for answer? Wait dp[0][1] recurrence uses dp[2][3], dp[1][3], dp[0][3] (column 3). Column 3 recurrence uses column 5, etc. So yes only odd columns. But what about dp[2][3] p=2, column 3; column 3 recurrence uses dp[4][5], dp[3][5], dp[2][5]. All odd columns. So answer depends only on odd k. But to compute odd columns, we don't need even columns. However, our 2D computes even columns too. Could optimize memory by computing only needed columns? But need dp[k+1][k+2] and dp[k][k+2] for odd k: k+2 odd, so column odd. Good. So we could compute only odd k columns. But initial dp[0][1] for n=1 has k=1 odd = n. For n=2, k=1 odd = n-1 base. For n=3, k=3 odd = n base, k=1 odd. So answer uses odd k. We could ignore even columns entirely. But is dp for even k ever needed to compute odd k? No, because recurrence increases k by 2, preserving parity. Thus even columns are irrelevant. Nice! We can reduce memory and time by half by only computing k with same parity as 1 (odd). But is that always true? Let's verify for n=4 (even): answer dp[0][1] uses column 3 (odd). Column 3 is base k=n-1 (since n=4, k=3=n-1) no recurrence. So only odd column. For n=5 (odd): answer uses column 3, then column 5 base. No even. For n=6 (even): answer uses column 3, then column 5 base (n-1=5). No even. For n=7: answer uses column 3,5,7 base. So indeed only odd columns. But wait, what about computing dp[0][1] for n=2: column 1 base. Good. For n=1: column 1 base. So we could compute only odd k from n down to 1. But base cases: if n is odd, k=n is odd, set dp[p][n]=nums[p]. If n is even, k=n is even, not needed; k=n-1 is odd base, set dp[p][n-1]=max(nums[p], nums[n-1]). Then for odd k <= n-2, recurrence. This would halve memory/time. But 2D with only odd columns still needs dp[p][k] for p<k for odd k. Number states ~n^2/4. Memory ~11MB. But code slightly more complex. Not necessary but could be beneficial. However, if we allocate full 2D and loop only odd k, we need ensure dependencies dp[k+1][k+2] and dp[k][k+2] are in column k+2 (odd) and set. Yes. We could implement:
```
for k in range(n if n%2 else n-1, 0, -2):
    if k == n:
       ...
    elif k == n-1:
       ...
    else:
       ...
return dp[0][1]
```
But for n even, start at n-1 (odd). For n odd, start at n (odd). Need handle n=1: start at1, k==n base. n=2: start at1, k==n-1 base. n=3: start at3, k==n base; then k=1 else. n=4: start at3, k==n-1 base. n=5: start at5 base; k=3 else; k=1 else. This works. But if we allocate full dp, we only fill odd columns. Even columns remain 0. Return dp[0][1] set. This reduces time to ~250k states. But is it always safe? Let's prove answer parity odd. Initial state p=0,k=1. Recurrence transitions to k+2, so k parity remains odd until base. Base occurs when k=n (if n odd) or k=n-1 (if n even). So all reachable states have odd k. Therefore we only need odd k. Extra even states are not reachable from initial and not needed. This is true. Could implement this optimized version. But the simpler full loop is easier to understand and less error-prone. Given n=1000, full is fine. But maybe memory limit unknown; optimized odd-only with full 2D still same memory if allocate full. Could allocate dictionary or 2D with only odd columns? Not necessary. If we want O(n^2) memory but fill half, memory still full due to allocation. We could allocate dp as list of lists with n+1 columns, but only fill odd; memory same. To reduce memory, we could allocate only odd columns? But code complexity.

Could implement O(n) memory by processing odd columns? Let's see if we can maintain only current column? Since dependencies only on column k+2 (same parity), if we process odd k descending by 2, we only need the previous odd column k+2. For each k, to compute dp[p][k] for p=0..k-1, we need:
- dp[k+1][k+2] and dp[k][k+2] from next column (two scalars)
- dp[p][k+2] for p=0..k-1 from next column.
So we can maintain an array `nxt` of length maybe n+1 representing dp[p][k+2] for p < k+2. Then compute `cur` for p<k. Then set nxt = cur for next k-2. But careful: `nxt` length should include p up to k+1 for current k. For next iteration k-2, we need dp[p][k] for p<k, so cur length k. This works. We can process odd k descending by 2. This would be O(n) memory and O(n^2/4) time. Let's explore if this is straightforward and maybe better.

We want compute dp[0][1]. Let's define a function to compute column k from column k+2. We can maintain `nxt` array where `nxt[p] = dp[p][k+2]` for p < k+2. For base highest column:
- If n odd, highest k = n. Column n base: dp[p][n] = nums[p] for p=0..n-1. So `nxt` length n (indices 0..n-1) representing dp[p][n].
- If n even, highest needed k = n-1. Column n-1 base: dp[p][n-1] = max(nums[p], nums[n-1]) for p=0..n-2. So `nxt` length n-1 representing dp[p][n-1].
Then for k = highest-2, highest-4, ..., 1:
  cur = [0]*k
  for p in range(k):
      cur[p] = min(
          max(nums[p], nums[k]) + nxt[k+1],   # dp[k+1][k+2] = nxt[k+1]
          max(nums[p], nums[k+1]) + nxt[k],    # dp[k][k+2] = nxt[k]
          max(nums[k], nums[k+1]) + nxt[p]     # dp[p][k+2] = nxt[p]
      )
  nxt = cur
Return nxt[0]? After processing k=1, cur length 1, nxt[0]=dp[0][1]. For n=1, highest k=1 base, nxt length1 = nums[0], return nxt[0]. For n=2, highest k=1 base, nxt length1 = max(nums[0],nums[1]), return nxt[0]. For n=3, highest k=3 base nxt length3 = nums[0..2]. Then k=1: cur[0] = min(max(nums0,nums1)+nxt[2] (dp[2][3]), max(nums0,nums2)+nxt[1] (dp[1][3]), max(nums1,nums2)+nxt[0] (dp[0][3])). Return cur[0]. This matches. For n=4, highest k=3 base nxt length3 = dp[p][3] = max(nums[p], nums[3]) for p=0..2. Then k=1: cur[0] = min(max(nums0,nums1)+nxt[2] (dp[2][3]), max(nums0,nums2)+nxt[1] (dp[1][3]), max(nums1,nums2)+nxt[0] (dp[0][3])). This matches answer for n=4. Wait for n=4, answer dp[0][1] uses dp[2][3], dp[1][3], dp[0][3]. nxt[2]=dp[2][3], nxt[1]=dp[1][3], nxt[0]=dp[0][3]. Good. We don't need compute k=2. For n=5, highest k=5 base nxt length5 = nums[0..4]. k=3: cur length3 = dp[p][3] for p=0..2:
cur[p] = min(max(nums[p],nums[3])+nxt[4] (dp[4][5]), max(nums[p],nums[4])+nxt[3] (dp[3][5]), max(nums[3],nums[4])+nxt[p] (dp[p][5])).
Then nxt=cur (column3). k=1: cur[0] = min(max(nums0,nums1)+nxt[2] (dp[2][3]), max(nums0,nums2)+nxt[1] (dp[1][3]), max(nums1,nums2)+nxt[0] (dp[0][3])). Return. This is O(n) memory and fewer states. This is elegant but slightly more complex. Need ensure for n even, highest k = n-1, and we skip k=n (even). For n odd, highest k = n. What about n=1: n odd, highest=1, base. Good. n=2: n even, highest=1, base. Good.

Let's verify with n=6 (even): highest k=5 base nxt length5 = dp[p][5] = max(nums[p], nums[5]) for p=0..4. Then k=3: cur length3 = dp[p][3] for p=0..2 using nxt column5. Then k=1: cur length1 using nxt column3. Return. This matches reachable odd columns. Good.

This O(n) memory version is nice and avoids large 2D. But is it always correct to ignore even k? Let's prove reachable states from initial (0,1) always have k odd because each operation increases k by 2. Base when k=n or n-1 depending parity. So yes. The recurrence for a reachable state never needs even k. Since we only need optimal cost from initial, we can restrict to reachable states. This is sound. The 2D full DP computed extra even states but not needed. The O(n) memory version computes only reachable states? It computes all odd k states p<k, including some not reachable? For odd k, p<k arbitrary; but recurrence uses them. Are all odd k states reachable? Maybe not, but they are valid hypothetical states with odd k. Do we need them? The recurrence for reachable states might use odd k states that are reachable? Let's see dp[0][1] uses dp[2][3], dp[1][3], dp[0][3]. These are reachable. dp[2][3] uses dp[4][5], dp[3][5], dp[2][5]. Reachable from [c,d,e]? State [c,d,e] p=2,k=3. Remove c,d keep e -> p=4,k=5; remove c,e keep d -> p=3,k=5; remove d,e keep c -> p=2,k=5. All reachable. So the recursion only visits reachable states. But our iterative odd-column computation computes all p<k for odd k, including maybe unreachable, but that's okay. It doesn't hurt. It is still O(n^2/4).

Could there be a reachable state with even k? Starting k=1 odd, each transition k->k+2, so no. Base if n even, final k=n-1 odd; if n odd, final k=n odd. So no even. Good.

Thus we can implement O(n) memory version. Which version should final code use? The 2D full is simpler and less likely to have off-by-one. The O(n) memory version is also simple but requires explaining? Code only, no explanation. Need ensure no bugs.

Let's design O(n) memory code carefully.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        if n % 2 == 1:
            # start at column n
            nxt = nums[:]  # dp[p][n] = nums[p] for p < n
            k = n
        else:
            # start at column n-1
            nxt = [max(nums[p], nums[n-1]) for p in range(n-1)]
            k = n - 1
        # Now nxt represents dp[p][k] for p < k, where k is the current odd column.
        while k > 1:
            # compute column k-2 from column k
            new_k = k - 2
            cur = [0] * new_k
            # For p in 0..new_k-1:
            # dp[p][new_k] = min(
            #   max(nums[p], nums[new_k]) + dp[new_k+1][new_k+2] = nxt[new_k+1]? Wait careful!
```
Need map variables. Let's denote current column `k` (higher), `nxt[p] = dp[p][k]` for p < k. We want compute `cur[p] = dp[p][k-2]` for p < k-2. Recurrence for column `k-2`:
dp[p][k-2] = min(
    max(nums[p], nums[k-2]) + dp[k-1][k],   # since (k-2)+2 = k; k+1? Wait if current lower column is L = k-2. Recurrence for dp[p][L]:
    max(nums[p], nums[L]) + dp[L+1][L+2] = dp[L+1][k] = dp[k-1][k]
    max(nums[p], nums[L+1]) + dp[L][L+2] = dp[L][k] = dp[k-2][k]
    max(nums[L], nums[L+1]) + dp[p][L+2] = dp[p][k]
)
So to compute column L=k-2, we need from `nxt` (column k):
- nxt[k-1] = dp[k-1][k]
- nxt[k-2] = dp[k-2][k]
- nxt[p] = dp[p][k] for p < k-2.
And nums[L]=nums[k-2], nums[L+1]=nums[k-1].
Thus:
```python
L = k - 2
cur = [0] * L
for p in range(L):
    cur[p] = min(
        max(nums[p], nums[L]) + nxt[L + 1],  # nxt[k-1]
        max(nums[p], nums[L + 1]) + nxt[L],  # nxt[k-2]
        max(nums[L], nums[L + 1]) + nxt[p]
    )
nxt = cur
k = L
```

Let's compare with 2D full recurrence variables to avoid confusion. In O(n) version, `nxt` is column k. To compute lower column L=k-2, recurrence:
- Remove nums[p] and nums[L], keep nums[L+1]. New state p'=L+1, k'=L+2=k. Cost max(nums[p], nums[L]) + dp[L+1][k] = nxt[L+1].

Reachable k parity:
Initial k=1. Each normal operation from state (p,k) with k<=n-2 transitions to k+2. So k remains odd. The process stops when k is n (if n odd) or n-1 (if n even) because those are base cases with fewer than 3 elements. More generally, if k > n-2? Base. Since k parity odd, the first base encountered descending from initial by +2 will be:
- If n odd: n is odd, so eventually k=n.
- If n even: n-1 is odd, so eventually k=n-1.
Thus we never need even k. Good.

Now, let's decide final code version. The O(n) memory version is shorter and more efficient. But is it too clever and risk of being considered wrong by a reader? The final answer is judged by code, not explanation. It is correct. Let's ensure no off-by-one in variable names.

Let's write O(n) code cleanly:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)

        # dp[p][k] is the answer for current list [nums[p]] + nums[k:].
        # k is always odd, so only odd k are needed.
        if n % 2 == 1:
            nxt = nums[:]          # column k = n: one element remains
            k = n
        else:
            nxt = [max(nums[p], nums[-1]) for p in range(n - 1)]  # column k = n-1: two elements remain
            k = n - 1

        while k > 1:
            new_k = k - 2
            cur = [0] * new_k
            for p in range(new_k):
                cur[p] = min(
                    max(nums[p], nums[new_k]) + nxt[new_k + 1],
                    max(nums[p], nums[new_k + 1]) + nxt[new_k],
                    max(nums[new_k], nums[new_k + 1]) + nxt[p],
                )
            nxt = cur
            k = new_k

        return nxt[0]
```

Let's test with n=1: n odd, nxt=nums[:1], k=1, while false, return nxt[0]. Good.
n=2: n even, nxt=[max(nums[0], nums[-1])], k=1, return. Good.
n=3: n odd, nxt=nums[:3], k=3, while k>1: new_k=1, cur=[0], p=0:
cur[0]=min(max(nums0,nums1)+nxt[2], max(nums0,nums2)+nxt[1], max(nums1,nums2)+nxt[0]). nxt[2]=nums2, nxt[1]=nums1, nxt[0]=nums0. Return. Good.
n=4: n even, nxt=[max(nums0,nums3), max(nums1,nums3), max(nums2,nums3)], k=3. while: new_k=1, cur[0]=min(max(nums0,nums1)+nxt[2], max(nums0,nums2)+nxt[1], max(nums1,nums2)+nxt[0]). Return. Good.
n=5: n odd, nxt=nums[:5], k=5. while new_k=3, cur length3:
p=0 uses nxt[4] (nums4), nxt[3] (nums3), nxt[0] (nums0). Good.
p=1 uses nxt[4], nxt[3], nxt[1]. Good.
p=2 uses nxt[4], nxt[3], nxt[2]. Good.
nxt=cur, k=3. while new_k=1, cur[0] uses nxt[2] (dp2,3), nxt[1] (dp1,3), nxt[0] (dp0,3). Return. Good.

Let's double-check the code indentation and syntax.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)

        if n % 2 == 1:
            nxt = nums[:]
            k = n
        else:
            nxt = [max(nums[p], nums[n - 1]) for p in range(n - 1)]
            k = n - 1

        while k > 1:
            new_k = k - 2
            cur = [0] * new_k
            for p in range(new_k):
                cur[p] = min(
                    max(nums[p], nums[new_k]) + nxt[new_k + 1],
                    max(nums[p], nums[new_k + 1]) + nxt[new_k],
                    max(nums[new_k], nums[new_k + 1]) + nxt[p],
                )
            nxt = cur
            k = new_k

        return nxt[0]
```

Let's maybe include a comment explaining `nxt` to make code self-contained. But final answer code block can include comments. The prompt says "Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block." Comments are code. I'll include maybe one comment at top of method:
```python
        # dp[p
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minCost(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)

        # State: current list is [nums[p]] + nums[k:].
        # k is always odd, so only odd k are reachable.
        if n % 2 == 1:
            nxt = nums[:]  # column k = n: one element remains
            k = n
        else:
            nxt = [max(nums[p], nums[n - 1]) for p in range(n - 1)]  # column k = n - 1: two elements remain
            k = n - 1

        while k > 1:
            new_k = k - 2
            cur = [0] * new_k

            for p in range(new_k):
                cur[p] = min(
                    max(nums[p], nums[new_k]) + nxt[new_k + 1],
                    max(nums[p], nums[new_k + 1]) + nxt[new_k],
                    max(nums[new_k], nums[new_k + 1]) + nxt[p],
                )

            nxt = cur
            k = new_k

        return nxt[0]
```
